70.54816pt\raise 5.70667pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.70667pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 94.63533pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.93747pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.9701pt\raise 5.70667pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.70667pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 140.63533pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.93747pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V_{c-1}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 198.89952pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.93747pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 165.22421pt\raise 7.2pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{J_{n}(\lambda)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 163.89954pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\large).

For simplicity, we let
v_{i}^{j}
be the basis vector
\omega(z_{i})^{(j)}
of
V_{i}
, and take also
v_{i}^{0}:=0
, so that
\alpha_{c}
-action on
V_{0}
is given by
v_{0}^{j}\alpha_{c}=v_{c-1}^{j-1}+\lambda v_{c-1}^{j}
. In the case when
n=1
, we will further omit the superscript index as long as there is no confusion. All arithmetic operations on the subscript index
v_{i}^{j}
will be taken modulo
c
.

Lemma 5.9
.

If
\omega=\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\omega)
, then the length
c
of
\omega
is even, say,
c=2r
, and for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,c\}
, we have
\sigma(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i+r}
and
\omega_{i+r}=\sigma(\alpha_{r})^{-1}
.

Proof.

Viewing the picture (
5.2
) as a
c
-gon by forgetting the orientation,
\sigma
acts as a non-identity element of the dihedral group
\langle\rho,\beta\mid\rho^{c}=1,\beta^{2}=1,\beta\rho\beta=\rho^{-1}\rangle
. Note that
\sigma
cannot act as the reflection
\beta
as it will fix at least a vertex of the
c
-gon.

Suppose on the contrary that
c
is odd, then
\sigma
acts as
\beta\rho^{k}
for some
k
as it is of order 2. But then
\sigma
will fix an edge of the
c
-gon, contradicting the fixed-point-free property of
\sigma
.

It remains to show that
\sigma
acts cyclically on the
c
-gon. Suppose the contrary, i.e. the source and target of the arrow
\alpha_{i}:=\sigma(\alpha_{c})
are
{i}
and
i-1
respectively. Since the source of
\sigma(\alpha_{c-1})
is given by applying
\sigma
on the target of
\alpha_{c-1}
(and vice versa), this means that
\sigma(\alpha_{c-j})=\alpha_{i-j}
for all
j
. Hence, there is some
1\leq k\leq i
such that
\sigma(\alpha_{k})=\alpha_{k}
, which contradicts the fixed-point-free property of
\sigma
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\omega
be a primitive closed curve on
\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}
and
n\geq 1
be a positive integer.
Then
\nabla M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})\cong M_{\mu}(\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\omega^{n}))
for some
\mu\in\{\lambda,\lambda^{-1}\}
; the same holds for the corresponding object
(\omega^{n},\lambda)\in\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, the
J
-module
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})
(respectively the coloured closed curve object
(\omega^{n},\lambda)\in\mathcal{C}
) is self-dual if and only if
\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\omega)=\omega
and
\lambda\in\{\pm 1\}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.12
, we only need to show for the
J
-module case.
Moreover, we only need to argue the case
n=1
as there are exact sequences

0\to M_{\lambda}(\omega)\to M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n+1})\to M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})\to 0

for all
n
that allows us to iteratively apply the exact equivalence
\nabla
to get the desired result for the case when
n>1
.

Similar to the construction of
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
. Let us consider a module
N_{\lambda}^{j}(\omega)
of the form

\textstyle{u_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{u_{j-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{u_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{u_{c-1}}
\textstyle{u_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}

where
\alpha_{j}
acts by multiplying
\lambda
.
Note that
M_{\lambda}(\omega)=N_{\lambda}^{c}(\omega)
.

We claim that, if both
\alpha_{j}
and
\alpha_{j-1}
points in the same direction (i.e. either both
\omega_{j}
and
\omega_{j-1}
are arrows or both are inverses of arrows), then
N_{\lambda}^{j}(\omega)\cong N_{\lambda}^{j-1}(\omega)
; otherwise,
N_{\lambda}^{j}(\omega)\cong N_{\lambda^{-1}}^{j-1}(\omega)
.
Indeed, it is enough to see locally that the commutative diagrams

\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Bbbk}

and

\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{-1}}
\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{-1}}
\textstyle{\Bbbk}
\textstyle{\Bbbk\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}

induce the required isomorphism.

Following the same argument in the proof of Lemma
5.8
(for the
J
-module case), we have
M_{\lambda}(\omega)=N_{\lambda}^{c}(\omega)\cong N_{\lambda}^{j}(\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\omega))
for some
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,c-1\}
. Note that
j\neq c
as
\sigma
is fixed-point-free on arrows. By the claim in the previous paragraph, we have
N_{\lambda}^{j}(\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\omega))\cong M_{\mu}(\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\omega))
for some
\mu\in\{\lambda,\lambda^{-1}\}
. This finishes the proof of the first assertion.

To show the second part, it remains to show that
M_{\pm 1}(\omega)
is self-dual when
\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\omega)=\omega
. By the argument of translating
\lambda
to a neighbouring arrow above, we only need to show that
\sigma(\alpha_{c})
and
\alpha_{c}
points in opposite direction, which is already shown in Lemma
5.9
.
∎

We are going to determine the exact values of
n
and
\lambda
so that
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})
has a structure of an
\varepsilon
-representation; hence, a 1-sided indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representation.
Note that by Proposition
5.4
, this implies that any other values of
n,\lambda
, the module
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})^{\oplus 2}
has a structure of a ramified indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representation.

From now on until the end of the section, unless otherwise specified, we will always assume the primitive closed
\omega=\sigma(\omega)
and
\lambda\in\{\pm 1\}
; otherwise, as
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
is not self-dual and so it must give rise to split
\varepsilon
-indecomposable.
We keep the symbol
c
for the length of
\omega
, and let
r
be
c/2
, which is an integer as guaranteed by Lemma
5.9
.

We will achieve our goal in two steps: Lemma
5.12
and Lemma
5.14
. The first one will tells us which
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})
is an
\varepsilon
-representation and the second one tells us that all other cases cannot have
\varepsilon
-representation structure.
Both steps will follow same strategy - we start by taking an
\varepsilon
-form
\langle-,-\rangle
(for arbitrary
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
) and use conditions (2) and (3) of Definition
5.1
to obtain a list of equations on the values
b_{i,l}^{j,k}:=\langle v_{i}^{j},v_{l}^{k}\rangle
, then we use these equations to determine the value of
\varepsilon
and whether the form is non-degenerate form.
For convenience, we also define
b_{i,l}^{j,k}:=0
whenever one of
j,k
is zero.

First, condition (2) of Definition
5.1
says that it is sufficient to consider only
b_{i,r+i}^{j,k}
.
Let us now write out the list of equations that condition (3) requires.
Recall from Lemma
5.9
that
\sigma(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{r+i}
for all
i\in\{1,2\ldots,c\}
.
If we consider an arrow
\alpha_{i}=\omega_{i}
, then
\sigma(\alpha_{i})=\omega_{r+i}^{-1}=\alpha_{r+i}
, and we have
\langle v_{i-1}^{j}\alpha_{i},v_{r+i}^{k}\rangle=-\langle v_{i-1}^{j},v_{r+i}^{k}\alpha_{r+i}\rangle
. Writing down similar equations for the cases when
\alpha_{i}\neq\omega_{i}
, and evaluating both sides of all these equations yields the following set of conditions indexed over
i\in\{1,2\ldots,c\}
and
j,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
:

(Eq:
i,j,k
)

\displaystyle\langle v_{i}^{j},v_{r+i}^{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle v_{i-1}^{j},v_{r+i-1}^{k}\rangle\quad\forall i\in\{1,2,\ldots,c-1\}\setminus\{r\}

(Eq:
r,j,k
)

\displaystyle\langle v_{r}^{j},v_{0}^{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle v_{r-1}^{j},v_{c-1}^{k-1}\rangle-\lambda\langle v_{r-1}^{j},v_{c-1}^{k}\rangle

(Eq:
c,j,k
)

\displaystyle\langle v_{0}^{j},v_{r}^{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle v_{c-1}^{j-1},v_{r-1}^{k}\rangle-\lambda\langle v_{c-1}^{j},v_{r-1}^{k}\rangle

For each
j,k
, going through (Eq:
i,j,k
) from
i=1
to
i=c-1
says that
(b_{i,r+i}^{j,k})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}}
is dependent only on
b^{j,k}:=b_{0,r}^{j,k}
, namely,

(5.5)

\displaystyle b_{i,r+i}^{j,k}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{i}b^{j,k},&\text{if $0\leq i<r$;}\\
(-1)^{i}(b^{j,k}\lambda+b^{j,k-1}),&\text{if $r\leq i<c$.}\\
\end{cases}

Putting this into (Eq:
c,j,k
) and rearranging the equation (also using
\lambda^{2}=1
and
c\in 2\mathbb{Z}
) yields

(Eq:
j,k
)

\displaystyle b^{j-1,k-1}

\displaystyle=\lambda(b^{j-1,k}+b^{j,k-1}).

Note that this is a null condition in the case when
j=k=1
, as (Eq:
c,1,1
) says only
b_{0,r}^{1,1}=-\lambda b_{c-1,r-1}^{1,1}
, which is already guaranteed by (
5.5
).

Summarising what we have so far:

Lemma 5.11
.

M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})
is a 1-sided indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representation if and only if there is a non-degenerate
\varepsilon
-form
\langle-,-\rangle
such that
b_{i,l}^{j,k}:=\langle v_{i}^{j},v_{l}^{k}\rangle=0
for all
l\neq r+i
, and the equations (
5.5
) and (
Eq:

j
,
k

) hold for all
i,j,k
.

Lemma 5.12
.

The following hold.

(1)

The indecomposable module
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
is an
\varepsilon
-representation if and only if
\varepsilon=(-1)^{r}\lambda
.

(2)

The indecomposable module
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{2})
is an
\varepsilon
-representation if and only if
\varepsilon=(-1)^{r+1}\lambda
.

Proof.

(a) We define a bilinear form so that its values on the basis is given by

\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle:=\begin{cases}(-1)^{i},&\text{if $i\in 0\leq i<r$ and $j=r+i$;}\\
(-1)^{i}\lambda,&\text{if $r\leq i<c$ and $j=r+i$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

It is straightforward to check that this is a non-degenerate
\varepsilon
-form for
\varepsilon=(-1)^{r}\lambda
.
Every bilinear form satisfying (
3.2
) will be a scalar multiple of the one here (which has
b^{1,1}=1
). The assertion follows.

(b) We first show the only-if direction.
By (
5.5
), we have
b_{r+i,i}^{2,1}=(-1)^{r+i}\lambda b^{2,1}
and
b_{i,r+i}^{1,2}=(-1)^{i}b^{1,2}
for all
0\leq i<r
.
If
j\neq k
, then (
Eq:

j
,
k

) says that
\lambda b^{1,1}=0
.
Now substituting
b^{1,1}=0
into (
Eq:

j
,
k

) for the case when
j=k=2
, we get that
b^{2,1}=-b^{1,2}
.
Hence, we have
b_{r+i,i}^{2,1}=(-1)^{r+i+1}\lambda b^{1,2}
for
0\leq i<r
.

By definition,
b_{i,l}^{j,k}
defines an
\varepsilon
-form if and only if
b_{i,r+i}^{1,2}=\varepsilon b_{r+i,i}^{2,1}
. Substituting the equations from the previous paragraph yields

(-1)^{i}b^{1,2}=b_{i,r+i}^{1,2}=\varepsilon b_{r+i,i}^{2,1}=\varepsilon(-1)^{r+i+1}\lambda b^{1,2},

i.e.
\varepsilon=(-1)^{r+1}\lambda
as claimed.

For the converse, define a bilinear form given by

\langle v_{i}^{j},v_{l}^{k}\rangle=b_{i,l}^{j,k}:=\begin{cases}(-1)^{i+j},&\text{if $j\neq k$ and $1\leq i\leq r$ and $l=r+i$;}\\
(-1)^{i+j}\lambda,&\text{if $j\neq k$ and $r<i\leq c$ and $l=r+i$;}\\
(-1)^{i+1}\lambda/2,&\text{if $j=k=2$ and $1\leq i\leq r$ and $l=r+i$;}\\
(-1)^{i+1}/2,&\text{if $j=k=2$ and $r<i\leq c$ and $l=r+i$;}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that here we have
b^{1,1}=0
,
b^{1,2}=1
,
b^{2,1}=-1
, and
b^{2,2}=\lambda/2
.

Again, it is straightforward to check that (
5.5
) holds.
As argued before for the only-if direction, (
Eq:

j
,
k

) yields
b^{1,1}=0
and
b^{2,1}=-b^{1,2}
- both of which are satisfied in our case.

Finally, as
\varepsilon=(-1)^{r+1}\lambda
, we have
b_{i,r+i}^{2,2}=(-1)^{r+1}\lambda b_{r+i,i}^{2,2}
for all
0\leq i<r
. Apply (
5.5
) to both sides yields

(-1)^{i}b^{2,2}=b_{i,r+i}^{2,2}=(-1)^{r+1}\lambda b_{r+i,i}^{2,2}=(-1)^{r+1+r+i}\lambda(\lambda b^{2,2}+b^{2,1}).

Since
\lambda^{2}=1
and
b^{2,1}=-b^{1,2}
, this equation rearranges to
b^{2,2}=b^{1,2}\lambda/2
. Hence, our bilinear form defines an
\varepsilon
-representation on
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{2})
.
∎

Now, we look at higher
n
.

Lemma 5.13
.

The equations (
5.6
) forces
b^{j,k}=0
for all
j+k\leq n
, and
b^{j,n+1-j}=(-1)^{j+1}b^{1,n}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
.

Proof.

For
j=1
, the equation (
Eq:

j
,
k

) says that
b^{1,k-1}=0
for all
1\leq k\leq n
; likewise, taking
k=1
in equation (
Eq:

j
,
k

) yields
b^{j-1,k}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq n
.
Now we go through the equations (
Eq:

j
,
k

) starting from the
j+k=4
(the equation for
j+k=2
is null and the equations for
j+k=3
are included in the previous sentence) to
j+k=n+1
.
For each fixed
\ell:=j+k\in\{4,\ldots,n+1\}
, if we iterate the equations (
Eq:

j
,
k

) from
j=1
to
j=\ell-1
, then in each iteration, we obtain
b^{j,k-1}=0
. Hence we have the first part of the assertion. In a similar way, taking
j+k=n+2
in (
Eq:

j
,
k

) yields
b^{j,n+1-j}=-b^{j-1,n+2-j}
, and the second part of the assertion follows.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

If
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})
is an
\varepsilon
-representation, then the following hold.

(1)

b^{1,n}\neq 0
.

(2)

\varepsilon=\lambda(-1)^{n+r+1}
.

(3)

n\leq 2
.

In particular,
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})\oplus\nabla(M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n}))
is a ramified
\varepsilon
-indecomposable for all
n\geq 3
and
\lambda\in\{\pm 1\}
.

Proof.

(a) Suppose the contrary. Then combining Lemma
5.13
yields
b^{1,k}=0
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. By (
5.5
), we then have
\langle v_{i}^{(1)},-\rangle=0
for all
i
. This contradicts the non-degeneracy of
\langle-,-\rangle
.

(b) Since
\langle-,-\rangle
is an
\varepsilon
-form, we have
b_{i,r+i}^{j,k}=\varepsilon b_{r+i,i}^{k,j}
. By (
5.5
), the case
0\leq i<r
yields

\displaystyle(-1)^{i}b^{j,k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(-1)^{r+i}(\lambda b^{k,j}+b^{k,j-1}),

(5.6)

\displaystyle\text{hence, }\quad b^{j,k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(-1)^{r}(\lambda b^{k,j}+b^{k,j-1}).

Note that if
j+k=n+1
, then
b^{j,k-1}
vanishes, so by Lemma
5.13
the equation (
5.6
) in these cases become
(-1)^{j+1}b^{1,n}=\varepsilon(-1)^{r}(\lambda(-1)^{n-j}b^{1,n})
, rearranging yields

\displaystyle b^{1,n}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\lambda(-1)^{n+r+1}b^{1,n}.

Hence, by (a) and the assumption that
\varepsilon,\lambda\in\{\pm 1\}
we get
\varepsilon=\lambda(-1)^{n+r+1}
as required.

(c) By rearranging equation (
5.6
) after substituting
\varepsilon=\lambda(-1)^{n+r+1}
from (b), we obtain

(-1)^{n+1}b^{j,k}+b^{k,j}+\lambda b^{k,j-1}=0.

Now consider the equation with
(j,k)=(2,n)
and with
(j,k)=(n,2)
.
1
1

1

Note that when
n=2
the two equations are the same, which is why the remaining of the proof does not work in this case.

When
n>2
, we can multiply the latter equation by
(-1)^{n+1}
and subtract it from the first equation, which yields

(-1)^{n}b^{2,n-1}+b^{n,1}=0.

On the other hand, by Lemma
5.13
, we can rewrite
b^{2,n-1}=-b^{1,n}
and
b^{n,1}=(-1)^{n+1}b^{1,n}
in terms of
b^{1,n}
, which means that we have
2(-1)^{n+1}b^{1,n}=0
. Hence, we deduce that
b^{1,n}=0
- a contradiction.
∎

Let us summarise our investigation so far. Recall (from the discussion after Lemma
5.8
) that the length
\mathrm{len}(\omega)
of a closed curve
\omega
is the number of intersections of
\omega
with the initial triangulation.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
(J=J_{Q,W},\sigma)
be a FF-symmetric gentle Jacobian algebra.
If
M
is an indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representation over
(J,\sigma)
, then exactly one of the following hold.

•

M
is a split
\varepsilon
-indecomposable with underlying
J
-module being either one of the following:

(SS)

M(\gamma)\oplus M(\sigma(\gamma))
for some curve
\gamma
with endpoints.

(SB)

M_{\lambda}(\delta)
for some (not necessarily primitive) closed curve
\delta
with
\sigma(\omega)\neq\omega
or
\lambda\neq\pm 1
.

•

M
is a 1-sided
\varepsilon
-indecomposable with underlying
J
-module being
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})
with
\omega=\sigma(\omega)
a primitive closed curve,
\lambda\in\{\pm 1\}
,
n\leq 2
, and
\varepsilon=(-1)^{\mathrm{len}(\omega)/2+n-1}\lambda
.

•

M
is a ramified
\varepsilon
-indecomposable.

Proof.

Proposition
5.4
that
M
is a split
\varepsilon
-indecomposable if and only if
M=N\oplus\nabla N
for some indecomposable non-self-dual
J
-module
N\ncong\nabla N
.
Thus, the first case follows from Lemma
5.8
and Lemma
5.10
, as they combine to say that non-self-dual indecomposable
J
-modules are precisely those that satisfy the condition (SS) or (SB).

On the other hand, Proposition
5.4
says that 1-sided
\varepsilon
-indecomposable are given by self-dual indecomposable
J
-module that can be equipped with an
\varepsilon
-representation structure. By Lemma
5.14
, such an indecomposable
J
-module must be of the form given in Lemma
5.12
. Now the rest of the claim follows.
∎

6.
Categorifying quasi-triangulations

In this section, we lift our work from Section
5
to the cluster category. Recall that when
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
is orientable with triangulation
T
, triangulations are categorified by
cluster-tilting objects
in the cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{T}
. We will formulate a symmetric representation theoretic analogue of cluster-tilting to categorify quasi-triangulations for the case when
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
is non-orientable.

We will use the same notations as in the previous section, that is:

•

(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
is a non-orientable unpunctured marked surface with
T
a triangulation on it.

•

\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}
is the double cover of
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
with Deck transformation group
\langle\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}\rangle
and
\widetilde{T}
be the induced double cover of
T
.

•

(Q,W,\sigma)
is the symmetric QP associated to
(\widetilde{T},\sigma_{\mathbf{S}})
,
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{C}_{(Q,W)}
is the associated cluster category, and
M(\cdot):\mathcal{C}\to\moduleCategory J
be the projection on the module category of
J=J_{Q,W}\cong\operatorname{End}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}(\Gamma)
, where
\Gamma
is the cluster-tilting object of
\mathcal{C}
given by
\widetilde{T}[-1]:=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\widetilde{T}}\gamma[-1]
.

•

\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
and by
\varepsilon
-representation we always mean
\varepsilon
-representation of
J
with respect to the involution
\sigma
.

6.1.
Lifting
\varepsilon
-representation theory to the cluster category

Before going into the definitions, note that an
\varepsilon
-representation
M
comes with a canonical isomorphism
\psi_{M}:\nabla M\xrightarrow{\sim}M
such that
\nabla(\psi_{M})=\varepsilon\psi_{M}
, and conversely, specifying such an isomorphism on an ordinary representation
M
is equivalent to specifying an
\varepsilon
-representation structure; see
[
23
, Sec 2.3]
. We can use this idea to lift symmetric representations to the cluster category
\mathcal{C}
as follows.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{(Q,W)}
and write
X=T^{\prime}[1]\oplus Y
a decomposition with
T^{\prime}[1]
the maximal direct summand of
X
in
\add(\widetilde{T}[1])
.

(1)

We say that
X
is an

\varepsilon
-object
if there is an isomorphism
\psi_{X}:\nabla X\xrightarrow{\sim}X
such that
\nabla\psi_{X}=\varepsilon\psi_{X}
. If, moreover, either one of the following cases occur:

•

T^{\prime}=0
and
M(Y)\in\moduleCategory J
is an indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representation of
J
,

•

Y=0
and
T^{\prime}=\nabla(\alpha)\oplus\alpha
for some arc
\alpha\in\widetilde{T}
,

then we call
X
an
indecomposable
\varepsilon
-object
(or
\varepsilon
-indecomposable for short). To distinguish the two cases, we will call them
non-initial
and
initial
respectively.

(2)

Suppose
X
is an indecomposable
\varepsilon
-object. The notion
split
,
1-sided
,
ramified
in Proposition
5.4
extends naturally to
\varepsilon
-indecomposable objects by regarding the case
X=T^{\prime}=\nabla(\alpha)\oplus\alpha
as split.

(3)

For an
\varepsilon
-indecomposable
X
, we define

\varepsilon
-factors
of
X
as follows.

•

If
X=\gamma\oplus\nabla(\gamma)
is split or ramified, then an
\varepsilon
-factor of
X
is either
\gamma
or
\nabla(\gamma)
.

•

If
X
is one-sided with
X=(\omega^{2},\lambda)
for some primitive closed curve
\omega=\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{c}
, then an
\varepsilon
-factor of
X
is
(\omega,\lambda)
.

•

If
X
is one-sided with
X=(\omega,\lambda)
for some primitive closed curve
\omega=\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{2r}
, then an
\varepsilon
-factor of
X
is a non-closed curve
\alpha=\omega_{i+1}\cdots\omega_{i+r-1}
so that
\omega_{i}
is an inverse arrow (or equivalently
\omega_{i+r}
is a direct arrow).

Example 6.2
.

Consider an initial arc
\alpha\in\widetilde{T}
.
Under the correspondence (
3.1
), we have an object
X:=\alpha\oplus\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\alpha)\in\mathcal{C}
, and the involution
\sigma
on
(Q,W)
induces an isomorphism
\psi_{\alpha}:\nabla(\alpha)\to\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\alpha)
.
Then we have

\psi_{X}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\psi_{\alpha}\\
\varepsilon\psi_{\sigma\alpha}&0\end{pmatrix}:\underbrace{\alpha\oplus\nabla\alpha}_{\nabla X}\to\underbrace{\sigma_{\mathbf{S}}(\alpha)\oplus\alpha}_{X}

that satisfies
\nabla(\psi_{X})=\varepsilon\psi_{X}
.
Every initial
\varepsilon
-object arise this way.

Let us give a more concrete example as well.

Example 6.3
.

Consider the quiver from Example
3.1
.
Let
S_{x}
denotes the simple module corresponding to vertex
x\in Q_{0}
.
Since
\nabla(S_{1})\cong S_{1^{\prime}}
,
S_{1}\oplus S_{1^{\prime}}
is a split
\varepsilon
-indecomposable representation, and we have an isomorphism
\psi_{M(X)}:S_{1}\oplus S_{1^{\prime}}\to S_{1}\oplus S_{1^{\prime}}
that satisfies
\nabla(\psi_{M(X)})=\varepsilon\psi_{M(X)}
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{C}
be the lift of
S_{1}\oplus S_{1^{\prime}}
, i.e.
M(X)=S_{1}\oplus S_{1^{\prime}}
, and
\psi_{X}\in\mathcal{C}
be the lift of
\psi_{M(X)}
. Then
(X,\psi_{X})
defines a split
\varepsilon
-indecomposable of string type.

Consider the band module
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
in Example
3.6
associated to the primitive band
\omega
.
By Theorem
5.15
, this is a 1-sided
\varepsilon
-indecomposable if
\lambda=\varepsilon
; ramified if
\lambda=-\varepsilon
. Hence,
(\omega,\varepsilon)
is a 1-sided
\varepsilon
-indecomposable object in
\mathcal{C}
and
(\omega,-\varepsilon)\oplus(\omega,-\varepsilon)
is a ramified
\varepsilon
-indecomposable object in
\mathcal{C}
.

As an application Theorem
5.15
, we have the following correspondences between curves on
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
with
\varepsilon
-indecomposables in
\mathcal{C}
.

Theorem 6.4
.

For every curve
\gamma\in\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
, fix a lift
\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathbf{C}\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}
.

(1)

If
\gamma\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
, then correspondence (
3.1
) induces the following bijection
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{curves connecting}\\
\text{marked points}\end{array}\right\}
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\varepsilon\text{-indecomposable objects}\\
\text{of split string type}\end{array}\right\}
1:1
\gamma
\widetilde{\gamma}\oplus\nabla\widetilde{\gamma}
.

(2)

If
\omega\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{cc}}^{1}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})=\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{1si}}^{1}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})\sqcup\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{2si}}^{1}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
is a primitive closed curve, then there are the following bijections
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{primitive 1-sided}\\
\text{closed curves}\end{array}\right\}
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\varepsilon\text{-indecomposable objects}\\
\text{of 1-sided primitive band type}\end{array}\right\}
1:1
\omega
(\widetilde{\omega},\varepsilon(-1)^{\mathrm{len}(\widetilde{\omega})/2})
,
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{primitive 2-sided}\\
\text{closed curves}\end{array}\right\}
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\varepsilon\text{-indecomposable objects}\\
\text{of ramified or split}\\
\text{primitive band type}\end{array}\right\}
\Big/\sim
1:1
\omega
[\,(\widetilde{\omega},\lambda)\oplus\nabla(\widetilde{\omega},\lambda)\,],

where the equivalence
\sim
in the last row is defined by
(\omega,\lambda)\oplus\nabla(\omega,\lambda)\sim(\omega^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\oplus\nabla(\omega^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
if
\omega^{\prime}
is one of
\omega
or
\nabla(\omega)
.

Note that the equivalence is effectively forgetting the colouring on the closed curves.

Proof.

Suppose
\overline{X}
is an indecomposable object in
\mathcal{C}
.
If
\overline{X}
is initial, then the
\overline{X}\oplus\nabla(\overline{X})
is an
\varepsilon
-object as discussed in Example
6.2
.
If
\overline{X}
is non-initial, then
M(\overline{X})
is an indecomposable
J
-module. This gives rise to an indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representation
M(X)
for some
X\in\mathcal{C}
, and hence an isomorphism
\psi_{M(X)}
in
\moduleCategory J
satisfying
\nabla(\psi_{M(X)})=\varepsilon\psi_{M(X)}
. Thus, by Proposition
4.12
, this lifts to an isomorphism
\psi_{X}
in
\mathcal{C}
satisfying
\nabla(\psi_{X})=\varepsilon\psi_{X}
.
Hence, non-initial
\varepsilon
-indecomposable objects in
\mathcal{C}
are in bijection with indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representations of
J
.

For indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representations of
J
that come from string modules, Theorem
5.15
says that they are always of the form
M(\gamma)\oplus M(\sigma(\gamma))
for some non-initial
\gamma\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{nc}}\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}\setminus\widetilde{T}
.
Since
\sigma
-orbits of
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{nc}}\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}\setminus\widetilde{T}
is just
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{nc}}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})\setminus T
, we get the bijection in (1).

Using the classification of one-sided indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representations in Theorem
5.15
and restricts to primitive bands, and the fact that
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{1si}}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
is the set of fixed points under
\sigma
in
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{cc}}^{1}\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}
, the bijection for
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{1si}}^{1}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
follows.

Finally, since
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{cc}}^{1}\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}/\sigma=\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{1si}}^{1}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})\sqcup\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{2si}}^{1}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
, the correspondence of the claim follows from the classification of ramified and split indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representations of the form
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
for primitive
\omega
in Theorem
5.15
.
∎

Recall that
\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(X,Y)
is defined as
\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y[1])
. Therefore, for each
f\in\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y[1])
, we have a triangle

Y\to C_{f}\to X\xrightarrow{f}Y[1]

in
\mathcal{C}
. So far as context is clear, we call the morphism
f
, the cocone
C_{f}
, as well as any isomorphic triangle an
extension from
X
to
Y
. Recall also that triangulations are in bijection with cluster-tilting (i.e. maximal rigid) objects of
\mathcal{C}
, and rigid objects are those with only split self-extension.

In the following, we give a ‘symmetric analogue’ of these notions.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
X,Y
be
\varepsilon
-objects in
\mathcal{C}
and
Y^{\prime}
be an
\varepsilon
-factor of
Y
.
We call
f\in\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y^{\prime}[1])
an

\varepsilon
-extension
from
X
to
Y
if
f\circ\psi_{X}\circ\nabla(f)=0
.
The case when
f=0
is called a
trivial
\varepsilon
-extension
.

We call
X

\varepsilon
-rigid
if every
\varepsilon
-extension from
X
to itself is trivial, i.e. for any
\varepsilon
-factor
X^{\prime}
of
X
, a morphism
f:X\to X^{\prime}[1]
satisfies
f\circ\psi_{X}\circ\nabla(f)=0
implies that
f=0
.

Remark 6.6
.

\varepsilon
-extensions are closed under scalar multiple, but unlike ordinary morphisms, they are not necessarily closed under addition.

Note that, if we consider the triangle
C_{f}\to X\xrightarrow{f}Y^{\prime}[1]\to
with
C_{f}
being the cocone of
f
, and consider also the long exact sequence induced by applying
\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}(\nabla(Y^{\prime})[-1],-)
to this triangle, then we can see that
f\circ\psi_{X}\circ\nabla(f)=0
is equivalent to saying that
\psi_{X}\circ\nabla(f)
factors through
C_{f}
. In particular, we have the following commutative diagram from the octahedral axiom of triangulated categories

\textstyle{\nabla(Y^{\prime})[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nabla(Y^{\prime})[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{X}\nabla(f)}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D^{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}[1]}
\textstyle{\nabla(Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nabla(Y^{\prime})}

where every row and every column is a triangle in
\mathcal{C}
.
We call this diagram the

\varepsilon
-extension diagram associated to
f
, and
E
the

\varepsilon
-extension from
X
to
Y
by abusing terminology (as in the classical case).

Since
M(-):\mathcal{C}\to\moduleCategory J
is a cohomological functor, to find
\varepsilon
-extensions from
X
to
Y
in
\mathcal{C}
, it suffices to find a commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(C_{f})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(Y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(D^{f})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{M(\nabla(Y^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\nabla(Y^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

in
\moduleCategory J
where all rows and columns are exact, with the first row being equivalent (as a short exact sequence) to the
\nabla
-dual of the right hand column. This is the strategy we will use to determine (non-)
\varepsilon
-rigidity throughout.

6.2.
\varepsilon
-rigidity for split and ramified
\varepsilon
-indecomposables

Lemma 6.7
.

Consider an
\varepsilon
-indecomposable object
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

(1)

In the case when
X
is a split
\varepsilon
-indecomposable, we have that
X
is
\varepsilon
-rigid if and only if it is rigid.

(2)

If
X
is a ramified
\varepsilon
-indecomposable, then
X
is never
\varepsilon
-rigid.

Proof.

(1) Write
X=\gamma\oplus\nabla(\gamma)
for an indecomposable object
\gamma
and so
\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(X,X)
is a direct sum of
\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\delta,\delta^{\prime})
over all
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in\{\gamma,\nabla(\gamma)\}
. If
X
is rigid, then all of these individual spaces is necessarily zero, so
X
is clearly
\varepsilon
-rigid.
Conversely, we have a non-zero morphism
f:\delta\to\delta^{\prime}[1]
for some
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in\{\gamma,\nabla(\gamma)\}
which yields
(f\pi)\psi_{X}\nabla(f\pi)=0
for
\pi:X\to\delta
the natural projection, and so
X
cannot be
\varepsilon
-rigid.

(2) If
X
is ramified, then
X\cong(\omega,\lambda)^{\oplus 2}
for some closed curve
\omega
and some
\lambda\in\Bbbk^{\times}
. But there is no rigid band object
(\omega,\lambda)
, so we must have a non-zero
f:(\omega,\lambda)\to(\omega,\lambda)[1]
, and apply the same argument as the converse part of (1) to see that
X
is not
\varepsilon
-rigid.
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose
\omega=\sigma(\omega)
is a primitive closed curve such that
(\omega^{2},\lambda)\in\mathcal{C}
is a 1-sided
\varepsilon
-indecomposable object. Then
X
is not
\varepsilon
-rigid.

Proof.

Recall that there is a functor
G_{\omega}:\moduleCategory\Bbbk[x,x^{-1}]\to\moduleCategory J
so that the full subcategory of modules of the form
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})
can be understood from the representation theory of
\Bbbk[x,x^{-1}]
-module; see for example from
[
32
, II.3, II.4]
.
We have
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})=G_{\omega}(V_{\lambda}^{n})
corresponds to the indecomposable
\Bbbk[x,x^{-1}]
-module
V_{\lambda}^{n}
where
x
acts as the
\lambda
-Jordan block of size
n
.
For any
n\geq 1
, let
v_{n}
be the generator of
V_{\lambda}^{n}
, and so
V_{\lambda}^{n}
have basis
\{(x-\lambda)^{k}v_{n}\}_{1\leq k\leq n}
.
Define
\iota_{n}:V_{\lambda}^{n}\to V_{\lambda}^{n+1}
to be the map
v_{n}\mapsto(x-\lambda)v_{n+1}
and
\pi_{n}:V_{\lambda}^{n+1}\to V_{\lambda}^{n}
to be the map
v_{n+1}\mapsto v_{n}
.
Let
\iota_{n,1}:=\iota_{n}\iota_{n-1}\cdots\iota_{1}
and
\pi_{1,n}:=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}
.
Then we have short exact sequences

\textstyle{\xi_{n}:\;\;0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{n,1}}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}
\textstyle{\zeta_{n}:\;\;0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{n}}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1,n}}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}
\textstyle{\rho_{n}:\;\;0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\pi_{n-1},\iota_{n})^{\top}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{n-1}\oplus V_{\lambda}^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt(\iota_{n},\pi_{n+1})}
\textstyle{V_{\lambda}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0;}

see, for example,
[
32
, Lemma II.4.2]
.
This induces the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\lambda}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\lambda}^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\lambda}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\lambda}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\lambda}^{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\lambda}^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\lambda}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

where the first row is
\xi_{2}
, second row is
\xi_{3}
, third right-hand column is
\zeta_{2}
, and left-hand column is
\zeta_{3}
. Note that the commutation on the top-right square comes from the sequence
\rho_{3}
.

Consider applying
G_{\omega}
to
\xi_{1}=\zeta_{1}=\rho_{1}
, we have a non-split short exact sequence

G(\xi_{1}):\quad 0\to M_{\lambda}(\omega)\to M_{\lambda}(\omega^{2})\to M_{\lambda}(\omega)\to 0,

which says that we can take
(\omega,\lambda)
to be an
\varepsilon
-factor of
(\omega^{2},\lambda)
.

By applying
G_{\omega}
to the commutative diagram of
\Bbbk[x,x^{-1}]
-modules above yields a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\lambda}(\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\lambda}(\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

with all rows and columns exact, and the commutation of the top-right square comes from
G_{\omega}(\rho_{3})
. Therefore, we can lift this diagram to an
\varepsilon
-extension diagram (as in Definition
6.5
) with
C_{f}=(\omega^{3},\lambda)\oplus t
,
D^{f}=(\omega^{3},\lambda)\oplus t^{\prime}
,
E=(\omega^{6},\lambda)\oplus t^{\prime\prime}
for some (possibly zero)
t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\in\add(\widetilde{T})
, we have that
(\omega^{2},\lambda)
is not
\varepsilon
-rigid.
∎

Example 6.9
.

Let
J
be the Kronecker algebra and
\omega
be the unique closed curve of the annulus.
Then
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{n})=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.68068pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.68068pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.722pt\raise 8.26941pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.68068pt\raise 3.01385pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.22415pt\raise-7.79167pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{J_{n}(\lambda)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.68068pt\raise-1.29167pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.68068pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V_{n}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
, where
U_{n}=\langle u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\rangle
and
V_{n}=\langle v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\rangle
over
\Bbbk
. We demonstrate that
M_{\lambda}(\omega^{2})
is not
\varepsilon
-rigid by showing the maps involved in the commutative diagram.
The first row

.

The rightmost column of the commutative diagram is given by the short exact sequence

The left column can be described similarly by enlarging the matrices similar to the previous paragraph.
Then one can check the commutative of the symmetric extension diagram explicitly.

Definition 6.10
.

An (oriented)
almost crossing from
\gamma
to
\delta
is an overlap (underlined and encapsulated by the
|
-separators) of the form

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\gamma}=&{[\gamma_{L}a_{L}^{-}]}\\
{\delta^{\prime}}=&{[\delta_{L}b_{L}]}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\kappa}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{[a_{R}\gamma_{R}]}\\
{[b_{R}^{-}\delta_{R}]}\end{array}\right.

where
\delta^{\prime}\in\{\delta,\delta^{-}\}
, each of the bracketed parts can possibly be empty,
\kappa
can be a trivial string, and
a_{L},a_{R},b_{L},b_{R}
are arrows.
We denote by
ac(\gamma,\delta)
the set of almost positive crossings from
\gamma
to
\delta
.

If all
a_{L},a_{R},b_{L},b_{R}
exists, then we get a genuine topological crossing between the corresponding curves; see Figure
10
.
If, for example,
a_{L}
(hence
\gamma_{L}
as well) does not exist in the almost crossing, then we should modify Figure
10
so that the curve
\gamma
starts from the marked point
m
instead.
In particular, if both
a_{L}
and
b_{L}
do not exist, then we get only an intersection of the curves at the marked point
m
.

\gamma
\delta
m
m^{\prime}
\kappa
a_{L}
b_{L}
a_{R}
b_{R}
f_{\mathtt{d}}

Figure 10
.
An oriented (almost) crossing
\mathtt{d}
from
\gamma
to
\delta

We remark that not every intersection (up to isotopy) between
\gamma,\delta
is encoded in
ac(\gamma,\delta)\cup ac(\delta,\gamma)
; the missing ones are called ‘crossing in a an arrow’ and ‘crossing in a 3-cycle’ in
[
33
]
.

6.2.1.
Homomorphisms between string modules

Let
\{x_{i}\}_{i=0,1,\ldots,c}
be the canonical basis of
M(\gamma)
, i.e.
x_{i}=\gamma(t_{i})
in the notation of Section
3
.

Likewise, let
\{y_{j}\}_{j\in J}
(for some finite indexing set
J
) be the basis elements of
M(\delta
).

If there is an overlap
\kappa=\gamma_{a}\cdots\gamma_{a+\ell}=\delta_{b}\cdots\delta_{b+\ell}
between the (string form of)
\gamma
and
\delta
, then we have
x_{a-1},y_{b-1}\in s(\gamma_{a})=s(\delta_{b}),x_{a+i},y_{b+i}\in t(\gamma_{a+i})=t(\delta_{b+i})
.
If
\kappa
is the overlap specified by
\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,\delta)
, we write
x_{i}\sim_{\mathtt{d}}y_{j}
when
x_{i}=x_{a+k}
and
y_{j}=y_{b+k}
for some
k\in\{-1,0,\ldots,\ell\}
. In this case, we have a module homomorphism

\displaystyle f_{\mathtt{d}}:M(\gamma)

\displaystyle\to M(\delta)

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}y_{j}&\text{ if $x_{i}\sim_{\mathtt{d}}y_{j}$;}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

We can regard
f_{\mathtt{d}}
as a counter-clockwise turning of subinterval
\omega
of the curve
\gamma
; see Figure
10
.

The following result of Crawley-Boevey says that
f_{\mathtt{d}}
defines a
canonical basis
of Hom-space between string modules.

Theorem 6.11
.

[
34
]

For two (possibly the same) strings
\gamma
and
\delta
, the set
\{f_{\mathtt{d}}\mid\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,\delta)\}
is a basis of
\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{J}(M(\gamma),M(\delta))
.

6.2.2.
Homomorphism between a string and a band module

A similar result exists for Hom-spaces involving band modules. In the scope of this article, we only need maps involving primitive band
\omega
, where the associated Hom-spaces have much simpler descriptions than the non-primitive ones. We refer the interested reader to the original paper of Krause
[
35
]
, or to
[
36
]
for which the following formulation is based on.

Suppose
\omega=\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{c}
is a primitive band.
Let
\{x_{i}\}_{i=0,2,\ldots,c-1}
be the basis of
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
where
x_{i}:=\omega(t_{i+1})^{(1)}\in s(\omega_{i+1})
in the notation of Section
3
.
We repeatedly self-concatenate
\omega
to form a bi-infinite string
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}:=\cdots\omega\omega\omega\cdots
.
This yields an infinite-dimension string module
M({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bbbk\hat{x}_{i}
and a surjection

\displaystyle\pi_{\omega}:M({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})

\displaystyle\to M_{\lambda}(\omega)

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{i-cm}

\displaystyle\mapsto\lambda^{m}x_{i}\quad\text{
for all $0\leq i<c$ and $m\in\mathbb{Z}$.}

The map
p:\omega_{i}\mapsto\omega_{i-c}
defines a
\langle p\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}
-action called
period-shifting
on
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
.
The definition of almost crossing extends naturally to bi-infinite string, in which case, the
\mathbb{Z}
-action on
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}
-action on
ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\delta)
and on
ac(\delta,{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})
for any (finite) string
\delta
.

As before, we take
\{y_{j}\}_{j\in J}
to be the basis of
M(\delta)
for a (finite) string
\delta
.
For an almost crossings
\mathtt{d}\in ac(\delta,{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})
, we have a homomorphism of modules

\displaystyle g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}:M(\delta)

\displaystyle\to M_{\lambda}(\omega)

\displaystyle y_{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi_{\omega}f_{\mathtt{d}}(y_{j})=\lambda^{m}x_{i}\text{ where }y_{j}\sim_{\mathtt{d}}\hat{x}_{i-cm}\text{ with }1\leq i\leq c\text{ and }m\in\mathbb{Z}.

We can write the
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}
in matrix form with respect to the canonical bases:

(6.1)

\displaystyle g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda^{d}f_{\mathtt{d},0}&\lambda^{d-1}f_{\mathtt{d},1}&\cdots&\lambda^{d-l}f_{\mathtt{d},l}&0\end{pmatrix}

where each
f_{\mathtt{d},m}
for
0\leq m\leq l
is (the
K
-linear map corresponding to) a
c
-by-
c
matrix and
f_{\mathtt{d},m}
for
0<m<l
are the identity matrices. Here the exponent
a
is determined by the smallest index
i-dc
with
0\leq i<c
and
x_{i-dc}\in\operatorname{Im}\nolimits(g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}})
.

One checks that
g_{p(\mathtt{d})}^{\mathrm{bs}}=\lambda g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. This means that the
\mathbb{Z}
-orbit
[\mathtt{d}]\in ac(\delta,{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})/\mathbb{Z}
determines the same map up to scalar multiple. Define
g_{[\mathtt{d}]}^{\mathrm{bs}}:=g_{\mathtt{d^{\prime}}}^{\mathrm{bs}}
where
\mathtt{d^{\prime}}
is determined by
y_{j}\sim_{\mathtt{d^{\prime}}}x_{i}
for some
0\leq i<c
.

Dually, for an almost crossings
\mathtt{d}\in ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\delta)
, we have a homomorphism
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}}:M_{\lambda}(\omega)\to M(\delta)
of modules so that
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}}\pi_{\omega}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda^{m}f_{p^{m}(\mathtt{d})}
, i.e. explicitly
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}}
is given by

\displaystyle g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}}:M_{\lambda}(\omega)

\displaystyle\to M(\delta)

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda^{m}f_{\mathtt{d}}(\hat{x}_{i+cm})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\hat{x}_{i+cm}\sim_{\mathtt{d}}y_{j}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{m}y_{j}.

Note that this is a finite sum as the overlap in
\mathtt{d}
is of finite length.
We can also write
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}}
in matrix form:

g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda^{d}f_{\mathtt{d},0}&\lambda^{d+1}f_{\mathtt{d},1}&\cdots&\lambda^{d+l}f_{\mathtt{d},l}&0\end{pmatrix}^{\top},

where each
f_{\mathtt{d},m}
for
0\leq m\leq l
are
c
-by-
c
matrix and
f_{\mathtt{d},m}
for
0<m<l
are the identity matrices.

It is straightforward to check that
g_{p(\mathtt{d})}^{\mathrm{sb}}=\lambda^{-1}g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}}
, so for a
\mathbb{Z}
-orbit
[\mathtt{d}]\in ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\delta)/\mathbb{Z}
, define
g_{[\mathtt{d}]}^{\mathrm{sb}}:=g_{\mathtt{d}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{sb}}
where
\mathtt{d}^{\prime}
is determined by
y_{j}\sim_{\mathtt{d^{\prime}}}x_{i}
for some
0\leq i<c
.

Theorem 6.12
.

[
35
]

For a string
\delta
, a band
\omega
, and
\lambda\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, we have

(1)

\{g_{[\mathtt{d}]}^{\mathrm{sb}}\mid[\mathtt{d}]\in ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\delta)/\mathbb{Z}\}
is a basis of
\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{J}(M_{\lambda}(\omega),M(\delta))
;

(2)

\{g_{[\mathtt{d}]}^{\mathrm{bs}}\mid[\mathtt{d}]\in ac(\delta,{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})/\mathbb{Z}\}
is a basis of
\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{J}(M(\delta),M_{\lambda}(\omega))
.

6.2.3.
Composition of homomorphism

Suppose we have three strings
\gamma,\delta,\eta
and two almost crossings
\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,\delta),\mathtt{e}\in ac(\delta,\eta)
. If the intersection of the overlaps of
\mathtt{d}
and
\mathtt{e}
is non-empty, then we have an almost crossing
\mathtt{e}\cdot\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,\delta)
; otherwise, we define
\mathtt{e}\cdot\mathtt{d}:=\emptyset
. It is straightforward from the definition of the canonical maps that
f_{\mathtt{e}}f_{\mathtt{d}}=f_{\mathtt{e}\cdot\mathtt{d}}
, where
f_{\emptyset}:=0
.

Consider
g_{\mathtt{e}}^{\mathrm{sb}}:M_{\lambda}(\omega)\to M(\delta)
and
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}:M(\gamma)\to M_{\lambda}(\omega)
. Then we have

(6.2)

\displaystyle g_{\mathtt{e}}^{\mathrm{sb}}g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}=g_{\mathtt{e}}^{\mathrm{sb}}\pi_{\omega}f_{\mathtt{d}}=\big(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda^{m}f_{p^{m}(\mathtt{e})}\big)f_{\mathtt{d}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda^{m}f_{p^{m}(\mathtt{e})\cdot\mathtt{d}}.

6.2.4.
Interaction with duality

We explain the effect of applying the duality
\nabla
to the canonical maps.
We will use the following convention. For a curve
\gamma
with string form
\gamma=a_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}a_{2}^{\varepsilon_{2}}\cdots a_{\ell}^{\varepsilon_{\ell}}
where
a_{i}
’s are all arrows and
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{+,-\}
for all
i
, we define

\nabla(\gamma):=\sigma(a_{1})^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots\sigma(a_{\ell})^{-\varepsilon_{\ell}}.

Let us first describe the isomorphism from
\nabla(M)
of an indecomposable string-or-band module
M
to the canonical basis of the resulting string-or-band module. For a string module
M(\gamma)
, we have defined the combinatorial operation
\nabla
on the string form of
\gamma
in a way so that there is an isomorphism
\psi_{M(\gamma)}:\nabla(M(\gamma))\xrightarrow{\sim}M(\nabla(\gamma))
.
Recall from subsection
3.3
that the canonical basis of
M(\gamma)
is given by
\{u_{i}:=\gamma(t_{i})\}_{0\leq i\leq c}
. Therefore, the module
\nabla(M(\gamma))
has a natural basis
\{u_{i}^{*}\}_{0\leq i\leq c}
dual to
\{u_{i}\}_{i}
. On the other hand, by design of
\nabla
(on strings), the canonical basis of
M(\nabla(\gamma))
is
\{v_{i}:=\sigma(\gamma)(t_{i})\}
. Under this setup, we have

\psi_{M(\gamma)}:\nabla(M(\gamma))\xrightarrow{\sim}M(\nabla(\gamma))\text{ given by }u_{i}^{*}\mapsto(-1)^{i}v_{i}\;\;\forall 0\leq i\leq c.

Let
E(\gamma):=M(\gamma)\oplus M(\nabla(\gamma))
, then we have an isomorphism

\psi_{E(\gamma)}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\psi_{M(\gamma)}\\
\varepsilon\psi_{M(\nabla(\gamma))}&0\end{bmatrix}:\nabla(M(\gamma))\oplus\nabla(M(\nabla(\gamma)))\xrightarrow{\sim}M(\gamma)\oplus M(\nabla(\gamma)).

This isomorphism is associated to the bilinear map defining the
\varepsilon
-representation structure on
E(\gamma)
(see discussion in
[
23
, Sec 2.3]
), that is,
\nabla(\psi_{E(\gamma)})=\psi_{E(\gamma)}
.

For a 1-sided indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representation
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
where
\omega=\sigma(\omega)
is a primitive closed curve (note that
\lambda=(-1)^{\mathrm{len}(\omega)/2}\varepsilon
by Lemma
5.12
), we have a similar isomorphism
\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}:\nabla(M_{\lambda}(\omega))\to M_{\lambda^{-1}}(\omega)
that satisfies
\nabla(\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)})=\varepsilon\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}
given as follows.

Lemma 6.13
.

Suppose that
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
is a 1-sided indecomposable
\varepsilon
-representation for some primitive
\omega=\sigma(\omega)
.
Let
\{u_{i}\}_{0\leq i<2r}
and
\{v_{i}\}_{0\leq i<2r}
be the canonical basis of
M_{\lambda^{-1}}(\omega)
and
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
respectively, i.e.
u_{i},v_{i}
are symbols given by
\omega(z_{i})
in the notation of subsection
3.4
.
Let
\{v_{i}^{*}\}_{0\leq i<c=2r}
be the standard basis of
\nabla(M_{\lambda}(\omega))
(whose underlying space is the
\Bbbk
-linear dual of
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
) dual to the canonical basis
\{v_{i}\}_{0\leq i<c=2r}
.
Then we have an isomorphism

\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}:\nabla(M_{\lambda}(\omega))\xrightarrow{\sim}M_{\lambda^{-1}}(\omega)\text{ given by }\begin{cases}v_{i}^{*}\mapsto(-1)^{i}u_{r+i}\\
v_{r+i}^{*}\mapsto(-1)^{i}\lambda u_{i}\end{cases}\text{ for all $0\leq i<r$}

such that
\nabla(\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)})=\varepsilon\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}
.

Proof.

The isomorphism
\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}
is the one naturally induced by the bilinear form defining the
\varepsilon
-representation on
M_{\lambda}(\omega)
, using the data from the proof of Lemma
5.12
. The fact that it satisfies
\nabla(\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)})=\varepsilon\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}
can be found in
[
23
, Sec 2.3]
.
∎

For any almost crossing
\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,\eta)
, say

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\gamma}=&{[\gamma_{L}a_{L}^{-}]}\\
{\eta^{\pm}}=&{[\eta_{L}b_{L}]}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\kappa}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{[a_{R}\gamma_{R}]}\\
{[b_{R}^{-}\eta_{R}]}\end{array}\right.,

its dual
\nabla(\mathtt{d})\in ac(\nabla(\gamma),\nabla(\eta))
is just the natural

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\nabla(\eta^{\pm})}=&{[\nabla(\eta_{L})\sigma(b_{L})^{-}]}\\
{\nabla(\gamma)}=&{[\nabla(\gamma_{L})\sigma(a_{L})]}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\nabla(\kappa)}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{[\sigma(b_{R})\nabla(\eta_{R})]}\\
{[\sigma(a_{R})^{-}\nabla(\gamma_{R})]}\end{array}\right..

In the case when
\eta={}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}=\cdots\omega_{i}\omega_{i+1}\cdots
for a
\sigma
-stable closed curve
\sigma(\omega)=\omega
, we identify
\nabla({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})
with the ‘half-period shift’ of
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
, i.e. if
\kappa\subset{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
starts at the
i
-th letter, then
\nabla(\kappa)\subset\nabla({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})
starts with the
(\mathrm{len}(\omega)/2+i)
-th letter.

Fix a
\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,\eta)
as above.
Let
a,b,\ell
be integers such that

\kappa=\gamma_{a+1}\gamma_{a+2}\cdots\gamma_{a+\ell}=\eta_{b+1}\eta_{b+2}\cdots\eta_{b+\ell}.

This means that, if we let
\{x_{i}\}_{i}
and
\{y_{j}\}_{j}
be the canonical basis of the indecomposable modules of
\gamma,\eta
respectively, then the canonical map sends
x_{i}
for
a\leq i\leq a+\ell
to a linear combination of
y_{j}
with
b\leq j\leq b+\ell
; here the indices are taken modulo
c:=\mathrm{len}(\omega)
when any one of
\gamma,\eta
is
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
.

Proposition 6.14
.

For strings
\gamma,\delta
, and primitive band whose underlying closed curve satisfies
\omega=\sigma(\omega)
, the following hold.

(1)

For any
\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,\delta)
, we have
\nabla(f_{\mathtt{d}})=(-1)^{a+b}\psi^{-1}_{M(\delta)}f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})}\psi_{M(\gamma)}
.

(2)

For any
\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})
, we have
\nabla(g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}})=(-1)^{a+b}\psi_{M(\delta)}^{-1}g_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})}^{\mathrm{sb}}\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}
.

(3)

For any
\mathtt{d}\in ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\delta)
, we have
\nabla(g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}})=(-1)^{a+b}\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}^{-1}g_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})}^{\mathrm{bs}}\psi_{M(\delta)}
.

Proof.

(a) This is straightforward to check from the definition of the
\psi
maps and
\nabla(\mathtt{d})
.

(b) Consider
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}
in its matrix form as in (
6.1
) but with each submatrix
f_{\mathtt{d},i}
decomposed further into a 2-by-2 block
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{\mathtt{d},i}^{L}&0\\
0&f_{\mathtt{d},i}^{R}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, each of size
r
-by-
r
for
r:=\mathrm{len}(\omega)/2
, that is,

g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda^{d}f_{\mathtt{d},0}^{L}&0&\lambda^{d-1}f_{\mathtt{d},1}^{L}&0&\cdots&\lambda^{d-l}f_{\mathtt{d},l}^{L}&0&0\\
0&0&\lambda^{d}f_{\mathtt{d},0}^{R}&0&\lambda^{d-1}f_{\mathtt{d},1}^{R}&\cdots&0&\lambda^{d-l}f_{\mathtt{d},l}^{R}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Since the overlap of
\nabla(\mathtt{d})
starts
r
places later than that of
\mathtt{d}
in
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
, the matrix form of
g_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})}^{\mathrm{sb}}
will be of the form:

g_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})}^{\mathrm{sb}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\lambda^{d}f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})_{R},0}\\
\lambda^{d-1}f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})_{L},1}&0\\
0&\lambda^{d-1}f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})_{R},1}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\lambda^{d-l}f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})_{R},l}\\
\lambda^{d-l-1}f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})_{R},l+1}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Note that here, for each
0\leq i\leq l
,
f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d}),i}^{R}
is the transpose of
f_{\mathtt{d},i}^{L}
and
f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d}),i+1}^{L}
is the transpose of
f_{\nabla(\mathtt{d}),i}^{R}X
. Also, the domain of
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{sb}}
is
M_{\lambda^{-1}}(\omega)
, and so the exponent on
\lambda
decreases as we go down the rows.

Now we compare the above matrix with
\psi_{M(\gamma)}\nabla(g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}})\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}^{-1}
.
As maps of vector spaces,
\nabla(g_{\mathtt{d}})^{\mathrm{bs}})
is just
\Bbbk
-linear dual of
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}
, so the corresponding matrix is just taking the transpose of
g_{\mathtt{d}}^{\mathrm{bs}}
. By the definition of
\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}^{-1}
, it swaps the two (block-)columns of the tranposed matrix, and then multiply
\lambda^{-1}
to the first column, and multiply a further
(-1)^{b}
to the resulting matrix. The effect of
\psi_{M(\gamma)}
further multiplies a factor of
(-1)^{a}
but does not permute any entries of the matrix. This results in the same matrix as
(-1)^{a+b}g_{\nabla(\mathtt{d})}^{\mathrm{sb}}
, as claimed.

(c) Follows from (b) by applying
\nabla
on both sides.
∎

Corollary 6.15
.

For any
\mathtt{d}\in ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\delta)
, we have
g_{[\mathtt{d}]}\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}\nabla(g_{[\mathtt{d}]}^{\mathrm{bs}})\neq 0
if
p^{m}(\mathtt{d})\cdot\nabla(\mathtt{d})\neq\emptyset
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

This follows from combining Proposition
6.14
with (
6.2
).
∎

Corollary 6.16
.

For any strings
\gamma,\delta,\eta
, and almost crossings
\mathtt{d}\in ac(\gamma,\delta),\mathtt{e}\in ac(\delta,\eta)
, we have
\nabla(\mathtt{e}\cdot\mathtt{d})=\nabla\mathtt{d}\cdot\nabla\mathtt{e}
.
In particular, we have

\nabla(f_{\mathtt{e}}\circ f_{\mathtt{d}})=\pm\psi_{M(\eta)}^{-1}f_{\nabla\mathtt{e}}f_{\nabla\mathtt{d}}\psi_{M(\gamma)}.

Proof.

First part is straightforward from definition of composition and
\nabla
-operation on almost crossings. The second part then follows by applying Proposition
6.14
(a).
∎

6.3.
\varepsilon
-rigidity for 1-sided
\varepsilon
-indecomposables

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
\omega=\sigma(\omega)
be a primitive closed curve and
(\omega,\lambda)
a 1-sided
\varepsilon
-indecomposable object.
If
\omega
is simple, then is
\varepsilon
-rigid.

Proof.

We need to show that every non-zero
f\in\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}((\omega,\lambda),\rho)
has
f\psi_{(\omega,\lambda)}\nabla(f)
for all
\varepsilon
-factor
\rho
of
(\omega,\lambda)
. Note that by Proposition
3.7
and
\tau
-invariance of band modules,
\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}((\omega,\lambda),\rho)\cong\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{J}(M_{\lambda}(\omega),\tau M(\rho)\oplus\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{J}(M(\rho),M_{\lambda}(\omega))
as vector space.

Write the underlying walk of
\omega
as
\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{r}\cdots\omega_{2r}
and
\omega_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{\varepsilon_{i}}
with
\alpha_{i}\in Q_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
for all
1\leq i\leq 2r
.
For the indices of letters or arrows in
\omega
, we will also use arithmetic modulo
2r
taking values in
\{1,\ldots,2r\}
.
Recall from Lemma
5.9
that
\sigma(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i+r}
for all
1\leq i\leq 2r
.
Let us fix an
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
so that
\rho=\omega_{i+1}\omega_{i+2}\cdots\omega_{i+r-1}
.
The form of the curves
\rho,\sigma(\rho),\omega
are shown in Figure
11
, where the dashed lines are supposed to be identified, the shaded parts represent the ‘outside’ of
\partial\mathbf{S}
, and two triangles are part of the triangulation.

\omega
\sigma(\rho)
\rho
\sigma(\alpha)
\tau
\alpha
\tau

Figure 11
.
The curves
\omega,\rho,\sigma(\rho),\tau(\rho)
on
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})

The first thing we claim is that
\dim_{\Bbbk}\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}((\omega,\lambda),\rho)=1
. It is a folklore that dimension of
\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
counts geometric intersection number (see
[
33
]
for related result for the case of intersections between two curves with endpoints), so the claim can be easily seen from picture (Figure
9
). Since the exact statement is not shown in the literature, we will do it explicitly here.

Claim 1
:
\dim_{\Bbbk}\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{J}(M(\rho),M_{\lambda}(\omega))=0
and
\dim_{\Bbbk}\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{J}(M_{\lambda}(\omega),\tau M(\rho))=1
.

Proof of Claim
:
By Theorem
6.12
, it suffices to show
ac(\rho,{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})=\emptyset
and
|ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\tau(\rho))/\mathbb{Z}|=1
, where
\tau(\rho)
is the string so that
\tau M(\rho)\cong M(\tau(\rho))
.

Since
\alpha_{i}^{-}\rho\alpha_{r+i}
is a subwalk of
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
, any almost crossing from
\rho
to
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
will induce an almost crossing from
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
to itself, which then means that the closed curve
\omega
has a self-crossing. Thus, the set
ac(\rho,{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty})
is empty.

For
ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\tau(\rho))
, first recall from
[
7
, Sec 3]
that
\tau(\rho)
can be described by moving the endpoints of
\rho
to the ‘next’ marked point on the boundary; see Figure
11
. In terms of strings,
\tau(\rho)=p_{L}\alpha_{i}^{-}\rho\alpha_{r+i}p_{R}^{-}
for some (maximal or trivial) paths
p_{L},p_{R}
; see Figure
12
. Hence, there are some
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,2r\}
so that we have an almost crossing

\mathtt{d}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}}=&{\omega_{L}\alpha_{j}^{-}}\\
{\tau(\rho)}=&\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{p_{L}\alpha_{i}^{-}\rho\alpha_{i+r}p_{R}^{-}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{\alpha_{k}\omega_{R}}\\
\end{array}\right.

in
ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\tau(\delta))
.
This is the only almost crossing (up to
\mathbb{Z}
-action) as otherwise we will have a almost self-crossing in
\omega
, which contradicts the simplicity assumption.
\blacksquare

\omega
\rho
\tau(\rho)
\cdots
\sigma(\alpha)=\alpha_{i}
p_{L}
\alpha_{j}

Figure 12
.
Understanding the string form of
\tau(\rho)

By Claim 1 and Proposition
3.7
,
\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}((\omega,\lambda),\rho)
is one-dimensional, and since the space of
\varepsilon
-extensions is closed under scalar multiple, it is enough to show for an arbitrary non-zero morphism
f\in\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}((\omega,\lambda),\rho)
satisfies
f\psi_{(\omega,\lambda)}\nabla(f)
. Since there is an equivalence
M(-):\mathcal{C}/[\widetilde{T}]\xrightarrow{\sim}\moduleCategory J
, it suffices to show that the canonical basis element
g_{\mathtt{d}}\in\operatorname{Hom}\nolimits_{J}(M_{\lambda}(\omega),\tau M(\rho))
for
\mathtt{d}
the unique (equivalence class of) almost crossing in
ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\tau(\rho))/\mathbb{Z}
that
g_{\mathtt{d}}\psi_{M_{\lambda}(\omega)}\nabla(g_{\mathtt{d}})\neq 0
. By Corollary
6.15
, it suffices to show that for the unique almost crossing
\mathtt{d}\in ac({}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty},\tau(\rho))
, there is some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
so that
\mathtt{d}\cdot\nabla(\mathtt{d})\neq\emptyset
.

If we index
\omega=(\omega_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
so that
\omega_{t}=\alpha_{\overline{t}}^{\varepsilon_{\overline{t}}}
, where
\overline{t}\in\{1,\ldots,2r\}
is
t
modulo
2r
, then the effect of applying
\nabla
to
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
takes
\omega_{t}=\alpha_{\overline{t}}^{\varepsilon_{\overline{t}}}
to
\omega_{r+t}=\alpha_{\overline{r+t}}^{-\varepsilon_{\overline{t}}}
.
Now the dual almost crossing is:

\nabla(\mathtt{d})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\nabla(\tau(\rho))}=&\\
{{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}}=&{\nabla(\omega_{L})\alpha_{r+j}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\nabla(p_{L})\alpha_{r+i}\nabla(\rho)\alpha_{i}\nabla(p_{R})}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}\\
{\alpha_{r+k}^{-}\nabla(\omega_{R}).}\end{array}\right.

Note that the
\alpha_{i}
appearing in
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
in the second line is
\omega_{2r+i}
, i.e. this
\alpha_{i}
appears in the ‘next copy of
\omega
in
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
’ relative to the
\alpha_{i}
in
\mathtt{d}
.
On the hand,
\alpha_{r+i}
in both
\mathtt{d}
and
\nabla(\mathtt{d})
are the same letter in
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
(namely,
\omega_{r+i}
).
Also, observe from Figure
12
that
p_{R}^{-}
is a prefix of
\nabla(\rho)
, and so
\nabla(\rho)=p_{R}^{-}\alpha_{k}q
for some walk
q
.
Dually, we have
\rho=q^{\prime}\alpha_{r+j}\nabla(p_{L})
.

Now combine all the information we have

\mathtt{d}\cdot\nabla(\mathtt{d})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\nabla(\tau(\rho))}=&\\
{\tau(\rho)}=&{p_{L}\alpha_{i}^{-}q^{\prime}\alpha_{r+j}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\nabla(p_{L})\alpha_{r+i}p_{R}^{-}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{\alpha_{k}q\alpha_{i}\nabla(p_{R}^{-})}\\
\end{array}\right.,

a well-defined almost crossing as required.
∎

The rest of this subsection is to show the following converse of Lemma
6.17
:

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
\omega=\sigma(\omega)
be a primitive closed curve and
(\omega,\lambda)
be a 1-sided
\varepsilon
-indecomposable object.
If
\omega
has self-intersection, then
(\omega,\lambda)
is not
\varepsilon
-rigid.

We write the
\omega=\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{2r}
where
\omega_{i}
are arrows or formal inverse of arrows. The fact that the length
2r
of
\omega
is even comes from Lemma
5.9
, which also says that
\nabla(\omega_{i})=\omega_{i+r}
.
Note that throughout, indices appearing in
\omega
(and its rotations and reflection) are always taken modulo
2r
.

An self-intersection of
\omega
is given by a(n almost) crossing:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\omega^{\prime}}=&{\xi^{\prime}_{L}b_{L}^{-}}\\
{\omega}=&{\xi_{L}a_{L}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\omega_{C}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{b_{R}\xi^{\prime}_{R}}\\
{a_{R}^{-}\xi_{R}}\end{array}\right.

where
\omega^{\prime}
is a primitive band obtained from some rotation of
\omega^{\kappa}
for some
\kappa\in\{+1,-1\}
, the symbols
a_{L},a_{R},b_{L},b_{R}
are arrows, and all other symbols are (possibly trivial) subwords.
Note that we do not need to work
{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}
and only focus on the primitive band since we are looking at self-intersection.

For all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, write
\omega_{k}=a_{k}^{\varepsilon_{i}}
for an arrow
a_{i}
and a sign
\varepsilon_{k}\in\{+1,-1\}
.
Let
i,i^{\prime},l
be integers so that

\displaystyle b_{L}^{-}\omega_{C}b_{R}

\displaystyle=\omega_{i^{\prime}}\omega_{i^{\prime}+\kappa}\cdots\omega_{i^{\prime}+\kappa l}=a_{i^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon_{j}}a_{i^{\prime}+\kappa}^{\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}+\kappa}}\cdots a_{i^{\prime}+\kappa l}^{\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}+\kappa l}},

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad a_{L}\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}

\displaystyle=\omega_{i}\omega_{i+1}\cdots\omega_{i+l}=a_{i}^{\varepsilon_{i}}a_{i+1}^{\varepsilon_{i+1}}\cdots a_{i+l}^{\varepsilon_{i+l}}.

In particular, we have
\varepsilon_{i}=+1=\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}+\kappa l}
, and
\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}}=-1=\varepsilon_{i+l}
.

The following result says that we can always assume the overlap of the crossing comes from two different copies of the subinterval
\omega_{C}
in
\omega
.

Lemma 6.19
.

Suppose that
\omega_{0}
is of minimal possible length.

Then
I^{\prime}:=\{i^{\prime}+\kappa,\ldots,i^{\prime}+\kappa l-1\}
and
I:=\{i+1,i+2,\ldots,i+l-1\}
are distinct intervals of
\mathbb{Z}/2r\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Clear the two intervals cannot be identical; otherwise,
\omega_{0}
is not an overlap of a crossing. This means that it is sufficient to show that
i^{\prime},i^{\prime}+\kappa l\notin I
and
i,i+l\notin I^{\prime}
. We can further reduce to showing
i^{\prime}\notin I
and
i\notin I^{\prime}
, as the other case follows by inverting both
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
. We present the argument for
i^{\prime}\notin I
; the argument for
i\notin I^{\prime}
is analogous.

Consider first the case when
\kappa=1
.
We have that
a_{i^{\prime}}^{-}
is a substring in
\omega
aligned with
a_{2i^{\prime}-i}
in
\omega^{\prime}
, and
a_{i+l}
is a substring in
\omega^{\prime}
aligned with
a_{i+l-i^{\prime}}
in
\omega
.
This means that the crossing is either of the form

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|ccccc|l}\omega^{\prime}=&\cdots a_{i^{\prime}}^{-}&\cdots&a_{i+l}^{-}&\cdots&a_{2i^{\prime}-i}^{-}&\cdots&a_{i^{\prime}+l}^{+}\cdots\\
\omega=&\cdots a_{i}^{+}&\cdots&a_{i+l-i^{\prime}}^{-}&\cdots&a_{i^{\prime}}^{-}&\cdots&a_{i+l}^{-}\cdots,\end{array}\right.

or of a similar form where the column indexed by
(i+l,i+l-i^{\prime})
appears on the right of the column indexed by
(2i^{\prime}-i,i^{\prime})
.

This implies that the substring
a_{i^{\prime}+1}^{\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}+1}}\cdots a_{i+l-1}^{\varepsilon_{i+l-1}}
of
\omega
coincides with the substring
a_{i+1}^{\varepsilon_{i+1}}\cdots a_{i+l-i^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon_{i+l-i^{\prime}}}
, and so we can find a new crossing:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\omega^{\prime}}=&{\cdots a_{2i^{\prime}-i}^{-}}\\
{\omega}=&{\cdots a_{i}^{+}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\cdots}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{a_{i^{\prime}+l}^{+}\cdots}\\
{a_{i+l-i^{\prime}}^{-}\cdots,}\end{array}\right.

where the overlap is of short length than the original one.
Hence, this contradicts the minimality assumption on
\omega_{0}
.

Consider now the case when
\kappa=-1
. If on the contrary that
i^{\prime}\in I
, then as indices decrease as we go right on
\omega^{\prime}
, the substring
a_{i^{\prime}}^{-\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}}}\subset\omega_{C}\subset\omega
will align with
a_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{i}}\subset\omega_{C}\subset\omega^{\prime}
. But this means that
\varepsilon_{i}=\varepsilon_{i^{\prime}}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma
6.19
says that, possibly after inverting
\omega
, we can write

(6.7)

\displaystyle\omega=b_{U}^{-}\omega_{C}^{\kappa}b_{V}\,\theta\,a_{L}\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}\,\theta^{\prime},

for some substring
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
such that
(U,V)=(L,R)
if
\kappa=1
; otherwise,
(U,V)=(R,L)
.
Note that the part
b_{V}\theta a_{L}
can possibly contract to a single letter
b_{V}=a_{L}
; likewise
a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}b_{U}
may contract to a single letter, i.e.
\omega=b_{U}^{-}\omega_{C}^{\kappa}b_{V}\theta a_{L}\omega_{C}
.

Since
\omega
is self-dual, applying
\nabla
to (
6.3
) yields a new self-crossing

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\omega}=&{\nabla(\xi_{L})\sigma(a_{L})^{-}}\\
{\omega^{\prime}}=&{\nabla(\phi_{L})\sigma(b_{L})}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\nabla(\omega_{C})}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{\sigma(a_{R})\nabla(\xi_{R})}\\
{\sigma(b_{R})^{-}\nabla(\phi_{R}).}\end{array}\right.

From this, one should expect that we can arrange the two copies of
\omega_{C}
in only half of
\omega
(and the other two copies of
\nabla(\omega_{C})
) in the other half; in particular, we should only have at most one of
b_{V}\theta a_{L}
and
a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}b_{U}
contracting to a single letter.

Lemma 6.20
.

There is a walk
\omega^{\prime\prime}
that is equivalent to
\omega
as a band, so that we can arrange both copies of
\omega_{C}
in
a_{L}\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}
and in
b_{U}^{-}\omega_{C}^{\kappa}b_{V}
to lie in an
\varepsilon
-factor
\rho
of
\omega
, namely, that

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=b_{U}^{-}\rho\sigma(b_{U})\nabla(\rho)

(6.12)

\displaystyle=b_{U}^{-}\underbrace{\omega_{C}^{\kappa}b_{V}\theta a_{L}\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}}_{\rho}\sigma(b_{U})\underbrace{\nabla(\omega_{C}^{\kappa})\sigma(b_{V})^{-}\nabla(\theta)\sigma(a_{L})^{-}\nabla(\omega_{C})\sigma(a_{R})\nabla(\theta^{\prime})}_{\nabla(\rho)},

for some substring
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
with the possibility that
b_{V}\theta a_{L}
contracts to
b_{V}=a_{L}
.

Proof.

Assume
\omega
is in the form of (
6.7
). Recall that
a_{L}
is positioned at the
i
-th letter in the walk
\omega
. If
i<r
, then we can just take
\omega^{\prime\prime}=\omega
.

Suppose that
i\geq r
, by Lemma
5.9
, we have
\sigma(b_{U})\nabla(\omega_{C}^{\kappa})\sigma(b_{V})^{-}=\omega_{r}\cdots\omega_{r+l}
and also
\sigma(a_{L})^{-}\nabla(\omega_{C})\sigma(a_{R})=\omega_{i-r}\cdots\omega_{i-r+l}
.
Note that by Lemma
6.19
,
a_{R}
must lie before the end of the walk
\omega
(i.e.
i+l<2r
).

For ease of reading, let
\beta:=b_{U}^{-}\omega_{C}b_{V}
and
\alpha:=a_{L}\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}
, so
\omega
is of the form

\omega=\beta\phi\nabla(\alpha)\phi^{\prime}\nabla(\beta)\nabla(\phi)\alpha\nabla(\phi^{\prime})

for some substring
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
.
By rotate
\omega^{-}
to a new primitive band that starts with
\beta^{-}
, we obtain

\omega^{\prime\prime}=\beta^{-}\nabla(\phi^{\prime})^{-}\alpha^{-}\nabla(\phi)^{-}\nabla(\beta)^{-}(\phi^{\prime})^{-}\nabla(\alpha)^{-}\phi^{-}.

The resulting band is of the form as claimed by swapping the role of
b_{U},\omega_{C},a_{L}
with
b_{V},\omega_{C}^{-},a_{R}
respectively.
∎

Let us rotate
\omega
again so that it takes the form

(6.13)

\displaystyle\omega=\begin{cases}\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}\nabla(b_{L}^{-}\rho)b_{L}^{-}\omega_{C}b_{R}\theta a_{L},&\text{if }\kappa=+1;\\
a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}\nabla(b_{R}^{-}\rho)b_{R}^{-}\omega_{C}^{-}b_{L}\theta a_{L}\omega_{C},&\text{if }\kappa=-1.\end{cases}

Consider the following string

(6.14)

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\omega\rho,&\text{if }\kappa=+1;\\
\rho^{-}\omega&\text{if }\kappa=-1.\end{cases}

We define another string
\delta
from
\nabla(\eta)
as follows

(6.15)

\displaystyle\nabla(\eta)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\nabla(\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime})b_{L}^{-}\omega_{C}b_{R}\theta a_{L}\overbrace{\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}\nabla(b_{L}^{-}\omega_{C}b_{R}\theta a_{L}\rho)}^{\delta}&\text{if }\kappa=+1;\\
&\\
\underbrace{\nabla(\rho^{-}a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime})b_{R}^{-}\omega_{C}b_{L}\theta a_{L}\omega_{C}}_{\delta}a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}\nabla(b_{R}^{-}\omega_{C}^{-}b_{L}\theta a_{L}\omega_{C})&\text{if }\kappa=-1.\end{cases}

We have now completed the setup needed.

Proof of Lemma
6.18
.

We prove the claim for the case when
\kappa=+1
by constructing the following commutative diagram where all rows and columns are exact sequences; for the case when
\kappa=-1
one just needs to modify the crossings
\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b},\mathtt{c},\mathtt{d},\mathtt{h}
.

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\mathtt{a}}-\lambda f_{\mathtt{b}}}
\textstyle{M(\eta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{\mathtt{c}}\\
f_{\mathtt{d}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{g_{\mathtt{h}}^{\mathrm{bs}}}
\textstyle{M_{\lambda}(\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{\nabla\mathtt{h}}^{\mathrm{sb}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\lambda f_{\mathtt{x}}\\

\textstyle{{\begin{array}[]{c}M(\delta)\\
\oplus\\

\scriptstyle{(\lambda f_{\nabla\mathtt{d}},f_{\nabla\mathtt{c}})}
\textstyle{M(\nabla(\eta))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{M(\nabla(\rho))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
