\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

For ease of reading, we use
\cdots
instead of writing the full walks as long as there is no confusion, and use a cross
\times
to denote an empty entry. To further reduce complication, we take

\phi_{A}:=\omega_{C}a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\phi_{B}:=\omega_{C}b_{R}\theta a_{L},

which means that

\rho=\phi_{B}\phi_{A},\;\;\;\omega=\phi_{A}\sigma(b_{L})\nabla(\phi_{B}\phi_{A})b_{L}^{-}\phi_{B},\;\;\;\text{ and }\delta=\phi_{A}\sigma(b_{L})\nabla(\phi_{B}\rho).

The almost crossings we needed are

\displaystyle\mathtt{a}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\phantom{\nabla()}\rho}=&{\times}\\
{\eta}=&{\cdots b_{R}\theta a_{L}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\rho}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{\times}\\
{\cdots\phantom{a},}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\phantom{\nabla()}\rho}=&{\times}\\
{\eta}=&{\times}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\omega_{C}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{b_{R}\theta a_{L}\phi_{A}}\\
{a_{R}^{-}\cdots\rho,}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\mathtt{c}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\phantom{\nabla()}\eta}=&{\times}\\
{\delta}=&{\times}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\phi_{A}\sigma(b_{L})\nabla(\phi_{B})\nabla(\omega_{C})}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{\sigma(a_{R})\cdots}\\
{\sigma(b_{R})^{-}\cdots,}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\eta}=&{\cdots\sigma(a_{L})^{-}}\\
{\nabla(\delta)}=&{\times}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\nabla(\delta)}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{\times}\\
{\times}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\mathtt{h}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\eta}=&{\times}\\
{{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}}=&{{}^{\infty}\omega\omega_{C}\cdots a_{L}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\omega\omega_{C}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{b_{R}\cdots}\\
{a_{R}^{-}\cdots a_{L}\omega^{\infty},}\end{array}\right.

These yield the following compositions:

\displaystyle\mathtt{c}\cdot\mathtt{a}

\displaystyle=0=\mathtt{d}\cdot\mathtt{b},

\displaystyle\mathtt{c}\cdot\mathtt{b}=:\mathtt{x}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\phantom{\nabla()}\rho}=&{\times}\\
{\delta}=&{\times}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\omega_{C}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{b_{R}\cdots}\\
{a_{R}^{-}\cdots,}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}\cdot\mathtt{a}=:\mathtt{y}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\rho}=&{\times}\\
{\nabla(\delta)}=&{\cdots a_{L}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\rho}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{\times}\\
{\times,}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\nabla\mathtt{c}\cdot\mathtt{y}=:\mathtt{w}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\rho}=&{\times}\\
{\nabla(\eta)}=&{\nabla(\phi_{A})b_{L}^{-}\omega_{C}b_{R}\theta a_{L}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\omega_{C}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{b_{R}^{-}\cdots}\\
{a_{R}^{-}\theta^{\prime}\nabla(b_{L}^{-}\phi_{B}\rho)}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathtt{d}\cdot\mathtt{x},

\displaystyle\mathtt{h}\cdot\mathtt{a}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\rho}=&{\times}\\
{{}^{\infty}{\omega}^{\infty}}=&{{}^{\infty}\omega\omega_{C}\cdots a_{L}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\omega_{C}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{b_{R}\theta a_{L}\phi_{A}}\\
{a_{R}^{-}\cdots a_{L}\omega^{\infty}}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=p^{-1}(\mathtt{h}\cdot\mathtt{b}),

\displaystyle\nabla\mathtt{h}\cdot\mathtt{h}=:\mathtt{u}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\eta}=&{\phi_{A}\sigma(b_{L})\nabla(\phi^{\prime})}\\
{\nabla(\eta)}=&{\times}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\nabla(\phi_{A})b_{L}^{-}\phi_{B}\omega_{C}}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{b_{R}\cdots}\\
{a_{R}^{-}\cdots}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathtt{c}\cdot\mathtt{d},

\displaystyle\mathtt{v}

\displaystyle:=p^{-1}(\nabla\mathtt{h})\cdot\mathtt{h}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rr|}{\eta}=&{\times}\\
{\nabla(\eta)}=&{\cdots a_{L}}\end{array}{\phantom{a}\underline{\phi_{A}\sigma(b_{L})\nabla(\phi_{B})\nabla(\omega_{C})}\phantom{a}}\begin{array}[]{|l}{\sigma(a_{R})\cdots}\\
{\sigma(b_{R})^{-}\nabla(\theta a_{L})\nabla(\phi_{A})}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathtt{d}\cdot\mathtt{c}.

From these datum (and that
\lambda=\lambda^{-1}
), it is easy to verify the commutation of the left-hand square and the bottom square, as well as the exactness of the second row and the left-hand column.

For the exactness of the first row, since
p(\mathtt{h}\cdot\mathtt{a})=\mathtt{h}\cdot\mathtt{b}
, we have

g_{\mathtt{h}}^{\mathrm{bs}}f_{\mathtt{b}}=g_{\mathtt{h}\cdot\mathtt{b}}^{\mathrm{bs}}=\lambda g_{\mathtt{h}\cdot\mathtt{a}}^{\mathrm{bs}}=\lambda g_{\mathtt{h}}^{\mathrm{bs}}f_{\mathtt{a}}

which yields the required exactness. The exactness of the right-hand column then follows by applying
\nabla
.

Finally, for the commutation of the upper right squares, by (
6.2
) we have
g_{\nabla\mathtt{h}}^{\mathrm{sb}}g_{\mathtt{h}}^{\mathrm{bs}}=\lambda^{-1}f_{\mathtt{v}}+f_{\mathtt{u}}
, whereas the composition through
M(\delta)\oplus M(\nabla(\delta))
yields
\lambda f_{\mathtt{v}}+f_{\mathtt{u}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

6.4.
\varepsilon
-cluster-tilting object

Definition 6.21
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{C}
be an
\varepsilon
-object with
\varepsilon
-indecomposable decomposition
X=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
.
We say that
X
is an

\varepsilon
-cluster-tilting object
in
\mathcal{C}
if

•

each
X_{i}
is an
\varepsilon
-rigid object,

•

\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(X_{i},X_{j})=0
,

•

X
is maximal with respect to the above properties, i.e. if
Y
is an indecomposable
\varepsilon
-rigid object
Y
satisfying
\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(Y,X_{i})=0=\operatorname{Ext}\nolimits^{1}(X_{i},Y)
for all
X_{i}\ncong Y
, then there must be some
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
so that
Y\cong X_{j}
.

Now we can collect everything we have in the section to obtain our main result.

Theorem 6.22
.

The bijections in Theorem
6.4
restricts to a correspondence

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{\otimes}(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\{\varepsilon\text{-rigid objects of }\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}}\}.

This induces a correspondence

\displaystyle\{\text{quasi-triangulations of }(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})\}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\{\varepsilon\text{-cluster-tilting objects of }\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}}\},

which restricts to a correspondence

\left\{\begin{matrix}\sigma\text{-stable triangulations}\\
\text{of }\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}\end{matrix}\right\}\leftrightarrow\left\{\begin{matrix}\text{triangulations}\\
\text{of }(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})\end{matrix}\right\}\leftrightarrow\left\{\begin{matrix}\nabla\text{-stable cluster-tilting}\\
\text{objects of }\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}}\end{matrix}\right\}.

Proof.

The first bijection follows from combining Lemma
6.7
, Lemma
6.8
, Lemma
6.18
, and Lemma
6.17
.
Now the second one follows from Proposition
3.8
.
In particular, when restricting to triangulations, we only get split
\varepsilon
-indecomposables appearing and so
\varepsilon
-rigid is just usual rigidity and
\varepsilon
-cluster-tilting is the usual cluster-tilting; these yields the last correspondence.
∎

Example 6.24
.

We consider
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
a genus 2 non-orientable surface with 1 boundary component and 1 marked point.
Let
T=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}\}
be a triangulation on
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
as shown in the middle of Figure
15
.
We display the gentle algebra as a quiver with relation on the left.
Here
i,i^{\prime}
for each
1\leq i\leq 4
are the vertices corresponding to the lifts
\widetilde{\alpha_{i}},\sigma(\widetilde{\alpha_{i}})
of
\alpha_{i}
to
\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}
, and the dotted line connecting two arrows represents a monomial quadratic relation given by the composition of the two arrows.

Consider the curves
\gamma,\delta
on
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
as shown on the right. Let
\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\delta}
be a lift to
\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}
.
Then

M(\widetilde{\gamma})\oplus\nabla M(\widetilde{\gamma})=1\oplus 1^{\prime},\text{ and }M(\widetilde{\delta})\oplus\nabla M(\widetilde{\gamma})=\begin{matrix}1\\
3\end{matrix}\oplus\begin{matrix}3^{\prime}\\
1^{\prime}\end{matrix}.

The set
\{\gamma,\delta,\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}\}
form a triangulation of
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
, and so the object

\widetilde{\gamma}\oplus\widetilde{\delta}\oplus\widetilde{\alpha_{2}}\oplus\widetilde{\alpha_{4}}\oplus\nabla(\widetilde{\gamma}\oplus\widetilde{\delta}\oplus\widetilde{\alpha_{2}}\oplus\widetilde{\alpha_{4}})

is an
\varepsilon
-cluster tilting object in
\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})}}
.
